(right).

∎

In this manuscript, Reidemeister moves in Subsection
4.1
and the Stabilization in Theorem
4.17
form the set of combinatorial moves that is available to us when manipulating an
N
-graph, if the Legendrian isotopy type of the associated (satellite) Legendrian weave is to be preserved.

4.8.
Legendrian Mutations

We now discuss the
N
-graph combinatorics of Legendrian mutations, a new geometric operation that we define in this manuscript. This operation inputs a Legendrian surface
\Lambda\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
and an isotropic 1-cycle
\gamma\subseteq\Lambda
, and outputs a Legendrian surface
\mu_{\gamma}(\Lambda)\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
. The Legendrian surface
\mu_{\gamma}(\Lambda)\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
will be ambiently (relatively) smoothly isotopic to
\Lambda
, and oftentimes not Legendrian isotopic to
\Lambda
. The choice of notation aims at emphasizing its relation to the wall-crossing phenomenon
[
GMN10
,
KS10
,
KS14
]
, Lagrangian mutation
[
Pol91
,
Aur07
,
Aur09
]
and
[
FOOO09
, Chapter 10]
, and quiver mutations
[
FZ02
,
Via14
]
.

Definition 4.18
.

Let
G\subseteq C
be an
N
-graph and
e\in G
and
i
-edge between two trivalent vertices. The mutation of
G
along
e
is the
N
-graph
\mu_{e}(G)
obtained by performing the exchange depicted in Figure
60
(left), also shown in Figure
4
(3).
\Box

By Theorem
4.21
below, the Legendrian weaves
\Lambda(G)
and
\Lambda(\mu_{e}(G))
will be mutation-equivalent, according to the upcoming
4.19
– this motivates Definition
4.18
from the perspective of contact topology. Note that the operation in Definition
4.18
is the simplest possible mutation, corresponding to the combinatorics associated to a Whitehead move, i.e. an edge flip in the context of triangulations dual to
2
-graphs. Indeed, consider the two unique non-degenerate triangulations
T_{1},T_{2}
of the square, the dual
2
-graphs
G_{1},G_{2}
differ precisely by a mutation along their unique internal edge.

Correspondingly, the standard satellites of their associated Legendrian weaves are two Legendrian cylinders with coinciding Legendrian boundary, smoothly isotopic relative to their boundary but which are
not
Legendrian isotopic relative to their boundary.

In general, given a
1
-cycle
\gamma\in\Lambda(G)
which is expressed combinatorially in
G
, it is possible to describe the mutation of
G
along such
1
-cycle
\gamma
. The mutated graph
\mu_{\gamma}(G)
can either be defined in an
ad hoc
way, or rather be understood as a graph which is equivalent via Reidemeister moves, as in Subsection
4.1
, to the mutated graph
\mu_{e}(\gamma)(G^{\prime})
. Here
G^{\prime}
is Reidemeister equivalent to
G
and
e(\gamma)
is an
i
-edge between trivalent vertices such that
[e(\gamma)]=[\gamma]\in H_{1}(\Lambda(G),\mathbb{Z})
under the canonical identification
H_{1}(\Lambda(G),\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{1}(\Lambda(G^{\prime}),\mathbb{Z})
given by a Legendrian isotopy. Here is the definition:

Definition 4.19

(Legendrian Mutation)
.

Two Legendrian surfaces
\Lambda_{0},\Lambda_{1}\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
are mutation-equivalent if and only if there exists a compactly supported Legendrian isotopy
\{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
relative to the boundary
\partial\Lambda_{0}
, with
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{0}=\Lambda_{0}
, and a Darboux ball
(B,\xi_{\text{st}})
such that

(i)

The two restrictions
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1}|_{(\mathbb{R}^{5}\setminus B)}=\Lambda_{1}|_{(\mathbb{R}^{5}\setminus B)}
coincide away from this Darboux ball,

(ii)

There exists a global front projection
\pi:\mathbb{R}^{5}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that each of the spatial fronts
\pi|_{B}(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{1})
and
\pi|_{B}(\Lambda_{1})
respectively coincide with each of the two fronts in Figure
59
.

\Box

Figure 59.
Legendrian mutation in a local spatial wavefront.

The two fronts depicted in Figure
59
coincide at their boundaries and lift to Legendrian cylinders. These Legendrian cylinders are
not
Legendrian isotopic relative to their boundary. Indeed, compactifying the upper sheet of the fronts with an
A_{2}
-cusp edge and a flat 2-disk, and the lower sheet with a different
A_{2}
-cusp edge and a flat 2-disk, yields the standard Legendrian unknot
\Lambda_{0}\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
for the left front in Figure
59
, and a loose Legendrian 2-sphere
\mathfrak{s}(\Lambda_{0})
for the right front in Figure
59
. The Legendrians
\Lambda_{0},\mathfrak{s}(\Lambda_{0})\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
are not Legendrian isotopic
[
EES05a
,
EES05b
]
.

A strong motivation for the study of the above mutations is the production of Legendrian surfaces which are
not
Legendrian isotopic, even though they belong to the same formal Legendrian isotopy class
[
Gro86
,
EM02
]
. In order to distinguish Legendrian isotopy classes we will be using
flag moduli spaces
, which synthesize Legendrian invariants coming from the study of microlocal sheaves in terms of algebraic geometry.

Remark 4.20
.

The conic Legendrian singularity for the front in Figure
59
(left) is
not
a generic singularity. It is explained in detail in
[
DR11
,
CM19
]
, and its generic perturbation contains four
A_{3}
-swallowtail singularities.
\Box

Theorem 4.21

(Legendrian Mutations)
.

Let
G_{1},G_{2}
be one of the pairs of
N
-graphs depicted in Figure
60
. Then the associated Legendrian surface
\Lambda(G_{1})
is a Legendrian mutation of
\Lambda(G_{2})
relative to their boundaries.

Figure 60
.
The Legendrian Mutation Moves in Theorem
4.21

Proof.

Let us start by showing that the exchange move in Figure
60
(left) corresponds to a Legendrian mutation,
as in Definition
4.19
. By
[
CMP19
, Theorem 6.3]
, the Lagrangian projections
\Pi(\Lambda_{0}),\Pi(\Lambda_{1})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{4}
of
the Legendrian lifts of the fronts
\pi(\Lambda_{0}),\pi(\Lambda_{1})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
in Figure
59
correspond to the two
Polterovich surgeries associated to the normal crossing of two Lagrangian planes
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0\},\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{4},\omega_{\text{st}})
.
The Lagrangian projection of the Legendrian lifts for each two
2
-graphs in the exchange move in Figure
60
(left) are exact Lagrangian
fillings
L_{1},L_{2}
of the Hopf link
\Lambda_{Hopf}\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{3},\xi_{\text{st}})\cong\partial(\mathbb{R}^{4},\omega_{\text{st}})
. Indeed, the 2-stranded braid word at the boundary of the 2-weave is
\sigma_{1}^{4}
, as there are four blue edges arriving at the boundary, and then note that the
(-1)
-framed closure of
\sigma_{1}^{4}
in
(\mathbb{R}^{3},\xi_{\text{st}})
is the Hopf link. See Section
7
for more details on Lagrangian fillings.
Thus, it suffices to show that
L_{1},L_{2}\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{4},\xi_{\text{st}})
are the positive and negative Polterovich surgeries of the two
Lagrangian planes
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0\},\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{4},\omega_{\text{st}})
at their intersection points.
Indeed, Figure
61
(center) depicts the 2-graph for the
singular
Legendrian whose Lagrangian projections is the Lagrangian union
(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0\})\cup(\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Figure 61.
The 2-graphs associated to a Legendrian Mutation. The middle 2-graph yields a spatial front which lifts to a
singular
Legendrian surface, consisting of the union of two 2-planes intersecting at a point.

The 2-graph in Figure
61
(center) describes a topological surface which is the union of 2-planes intersecting at a point, both for the Lagrangian surfaces in
(\mathbb{R}^{4},\omega_{\text{st}})

and
the Legendrian surfaces in
(\mathbb{R}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
. Topologically, the front in Figure

(center) is the cone over the annular projection of the
(2,4)
-braid, with singular crossings
15
15

15

This is consistent with the fact that the Hopf link is the boundary of two transversely intersecting planes in the 4-ball
\mathbb{D}^{4}
. For the max-tb
Legendrian
Hopf link, these two planes should be taken to be Lagrangian.
.

Finally, the Lagrangian projections of the Legendrian lifts of Figure

(left) and Figure

(right) are realized as Polterovich surgeries of the corresponding Lagrangian projection in Figure

(center). Since the Legendrian lifts of Polterovich surgeries are Legendrian mutations
[

, this concludes the first part of Theorem
4.21
.

Let us now show that the exchange move in Figure
60
(right) also corresponds to a unique Legendrian mutation. This is proven directly through the homotopy of fronts in Figure
62
.

Indeed, the first step in Figure
62
, starting from the upper left, consists of applying Move II, pushing a trivalent vertex through a hexagonal vertex. The second and third steps are also a direct application of a Move II, pushing the remaining two trivalent vertices through the newly created
two
hexagonal vertices. The fourth move, starting at the left of the second row, is a mutation of 2-graphs. This yields the 3-graph at the center of the second row, the arrow being labeled by the letter
\mu
. Finally, we apply a Move III, flopping the four vertices nearest to the center, in order to achieve the 3-graph at the right of Figure
60
(right). This shows that the exchange move in Figure
60
(right) is a Legendrian mutation.

Figure 62.
The Legendrian mutation for
3
-graphs as a sequence of Legendrian isotopies and
2
-graph mutation.

∎

For our applications to Lagrangian fillings, it is important to understand how 1-cycle representatives of classes in

change under the mutations depicted in Figure

\sf Y

.

Let

\gamma

Theorem
4.21
and Corollary
4.22
describe mutations along
\sf Y
-cycles and
\sf I
-cycles, either monochromatic or long edges. In general, we might be interested in mutating along a cycle
\gamma

\sf Y

\sf I
-piece, as introduced in Section

. Thus, we now develop
local
rules for Legendrian mutations that will allow us to mutation along any such cycle
\gamma
. These rules also imply Corollary
4.22
. All these rules are obtained and proven in the same manner: one simplifies the weave with equivalence moves – using Section
4
– until the cycle to be mutated becomes a
short

\sf I
-cycle. Then we apply the short
\sf I
-cycle mutation in Figure
60
(left) and rearrange the weave with moves to the required configuration. For instance, for Corollary
4.22
(and so Figure

), we push-through the left-most trivalent vertex through all the hexagonal vertices until the long
\sf I
-cycle becomes a short
\sf I
-cycle. Then we mutate at the short cycle, and push-through one of the trivalent vertices back to the left.

4.10.
Sufficiency For Stabilized Legendrians

Finally, we conclude this section by introducing the following combinatorial idea, motivated by the topology of Legendrian surfaces in 5-dimensional contact manifolds.

Definition 4.23
.

An
N
-graph
G\subseteq C
is said to have a
bridge
if there exists two disjoint 2-disks
D_{1},D_{2}\subseteq C
such that the complement
G\setminus(G\cap D_{1}\cup G\cap D_{2})
consists of
(N-1)
disjoint strands with labels
\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\ldots,\tau_{N-1}
consecutive with respect to a transverse oriented curve in
C\setminus(D_{1}\cup D_{2})
.
\Box

For the
N=2
case, where
G
is a trivalent graph, a bridge for
G
according to Definition
4.23
coincides with the standard graph-theoretic notion of a bridge
[
BM08
,
Die17
]
. A general
N
-graph
G\subseteq C
with a bridge is depicted in Figure
72
(left), and an example of a
4
-graph with a bridge is shown in Figure
72
(right).

Figure 72.
Structure of an
N
-graph with a bridge (left) and instance of a
4
-graph with a bridge (right).

The geometric motivation for this definition is based on the theory of loose Legendrian surfaces, also known as stabilized Legendrians
[
Mur12
]
. This class of loose Legendrians are known to satisfy an
h
-principle and has proven to be very useful in the study of Weinstein manifolds
[
CE12
,
CM19
]
. The reader is referred to
[
CE12
,
Mur12
]
for further details. For the present manuscript, we will assume known its definition and state the following property:

Proposition 4.24
.

Let
G\subseteq C
be an
N
-graph with a bridge. Then
\iota(\Lambda(G))
is a loose Legendrian surface.

Proof.

The proof is a simple argument in the theory of spatial fronts. Indeed, consider the 1-dimensional front slice along the dashed orange line in Figure
72
. The braid shown along this slice is depicted in Figure
73
(left). Its closure as a satellite of the standard Legendrian unknot is shown in Figure
73
(center). This Legendrian link is isotopic, via a sequence of Reidemeister II moves, to the Legendrian link given by the front in Figure
73
(right). The loose chart is exhibited in yellow in this figure. Note that this chart has arbitrarily large thickness due to the dilation freedom in
(\mathbb{R}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
and the fact that our front is global. This proves that
\iota(\Lambda(G))
is a loose Legendrian if
G
has a bridge.
∎

Figure 73.
The front for the Legendrian link obtained in a 3-dimensional slice of a bridge (left). The front for the corresponding satellite closure (center) and a homotopic front exhibiting a loose chart (right).

Proposition
4.24
immediately has the following consequence.

Corollary 4.25
.

Let
G\subseteq C
be an
N
-graph with a bridge. Then
\iota(\Lambda(G))\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
admits no exact Lagrangian filling
L\subseteq(\mathbb{D}^{6},\omega_{\text{st}})
.
\Box

Corollary
4.25
should be contrasted with the fact that many of the Legendrian surfaces
\iota(\Lambda(G))\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
admit exact Lagrangian fillings. For instance, it follows from Theorem
4.10
that any 2-graph
G
obtained from the unique two-vertex 2-graph by adding
bigons
, i.e. a 1-surgery, yields a Legendrian surface
\iota(\Lambda(G)))
which admits exact Lagrangian fillings. On the other hand,
simple
2-graphs do not – see
[
TZ18
, Theorem 1.3]
.

Example 4.26

(Exact Lagrangian Cobordisms To a Loose Legendrian)
.

Consider the Legendrian Clifford 2-torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{c}\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
associated, via the standard satellite, to the 2-graph in Figure
74
(Left). By applying our combinatorial Legendrian surgery from Theorem
4.10
, Figure
43
.(2), we obtain an (index-2) exact Lagrangian cobordism from
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{c}
to the Legendrian 2-sphere
\Lambda_{l}
associated Figure
74
(Right). By Proposition
4.24
, the Legendrian
\Lambda_{l}
is a loose Legendrian surface. This proves that the Legendrian Clifford 2-torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{c}\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
is a subloose Legendrian surface, and we will show in Section
6
that
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{c}
is
not
a loose Legendrian. In particular, this also proves that
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{c}\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
admits no 3-dimensional exact Lagrangian fillings
L\subseteq(\mathbb{D}^{6},\lambda_{\text{st}})
in the standard symplectic 6-disk. The points in the non-empty flag moduli associated to
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{c}
will in fact be geometrically represented by
non
-exact Lagrangian fillings.
\Box

Figure 74.
An exact Lagrangian cobordism from a non-loose Legendrian 2-torus to a loose Legendrian 2-sphere.

5.
Flag Moduli Spaces

In this section we introduce one of the central algebraic invariants in this article, the flag
16
16

16

“Vexillary” is the appropriate adjectival form of “flag”. Hence, it should technically be named the
vexillary
moduli space. The word is possibly too obscure, and we thus favor
flag
moduli space, as in
flag
variety.
moduli space
\mathcal{M}(G)
of an
N
-graph
G
and its associated Legendrian weave. We will prove that these flag moduli spaces are moduli spaces of constructible sheaves associated to a Legendrian weave, but we first present their explicit and self-contained definition.

5.1.
Preliminaries on the Flag Variety

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be a natural number and
R
a commutative ground ring, which will oftentimes be a field. We denote by
\operatorname{GL}_{N}
the general linear group, a scheme whose value over
R
is
\operatorname{GL}(N,R)
, and likewise for
\operatorname{PGL}_{N}
, the projective general linear group. By definition, a (full or complete) flag is an element

\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}\in\{\mathscr{F}^{0}\subset\mathscr{F}^{1}\subset\mathscr{F}^{2}\subset\cdots\subset\mathscr{F}^{N-1}\subset\mathscr{F}^{N}:\mbox{dim}\mathscr{F}^{i}=i,0\leq i\leq N\},

i.e. a sequence of nested linear subspaces
\mathscr{F}^{i}\subseteq R^{N}=R\oplus\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(N)}}{{\ldots}}\oplus R
,
0\leq i\leq N
. Let
B\subseteq GL_{N}
be the Borel subgroup
17
17

17

This is a maximal Zariski closed and connected solvable algebraic subgroup. Since
B
is a minimal parabolic subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{N}
it preserves the
most
geometric linear structure in
R^{N}
, which is precisely a flag
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}
.
of upper triangular matrices preserving the standard coordinate flag. Since
GL_{N}
acts transitively on the set of bases, the space that parametrizes such full flags is the homogeneous space
\mathcal{B}=\operatorname{GL}_{N}/B
. This is an algebraic variety, known as the flag variety.

The relative position of two flags
(\mathscr{F}^{\bullet},\mathscr{G}^{\bullet})\in\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}
is encoded algebraically by the Bruhat decomposition

\operatorname{GL}_{N}=\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{w\in S_{N}}BwB,

where the symmetric group
S_{N}=W(\operatorname{GL}_{N})
is identified with the Weyl group. That is, the orbits of the diagonal action of
\operatorname{GL}_{N}
on a pair of flags are indexed by the symmetric group
S_{N}.
The dimension of the Bruhat cell
BwB
is the length
\ell(w)
of the permutation
w\in S_{n}
. By definition,
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}
and
\mathscr{G}^{\bullet}
are in
transverse
position (or
totally transverse
or
completely transverse
) if their relative position is
w_{0}\in S_{N}
, where
w_{0}
denotes the longest element in the Coxeter group
S_{N}
. Note that
\ell(w_{0})={N\choose 2}
,
w_{0}\in S_{N}
, and that
totally transverse
is the generic relative position between two points in the flag variety
\mathcal{B}
.
In particular, an elementary transposition
\tau_{i}\in S_{N}
determines a relative position between two flags
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}
and
\mathscr{G}^{\bullet}
in which only their
i
th vector spaces differ, and no others.

We will require a slight generalization of the above when the surface
C
is not simply connected: compatible local systems of flags, rather than flags of subspaces of a fixed vector space.
This will not be required for our applications in Sections
6
,
7
and
8
, so the reader is welcome to skip this paragraph. Let
E\longrightarrow X
be a local system on a topological space
X
. By a local system of flags, we mean a complete filtration (flag)
\mathscr{E}^{\bullet}
of
E
by local systems
\mathscr{E}^{k}
such that the monodromy preserves the filtration. In this sense, the flag itself makes global sense.
Let
U\subset X
be a subspace and let
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}
be a flag of sub-local systems on
U
, so that
\mathscr{F}^{k}\subseteq E
for all
0\leq k\leq N
. We say that
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}
is
compatible
with
\mathscr{E}^{\bullet}
if the monodromies are: specifically, for
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(U,u)
and
v\in\mathscr{F}^{k}
,
i_{k}(\gamma\cdot v)=i_{k,*}(\gamma)\cdot i_{k}(v),
where the symbol
\cdot
denotes (ambiguously) the action of any group on a vector space.
Note that by monodromy invariance, we may speak of the relative position of two compatible sub-local systems of flags
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}
and
\mathscr{G}^{\bullet}
on subspaces
U
and
U^{\prime}
of
X
.

With these algebraic preliminaries, we turn to describing the flag moduli space associated to an
N
-graph.

5.2.
Description of the Flag Moduli Space of an
N
-graph

Let
G
be an
N
-graph on a connected surface
C
, thought of as the union of the embedded graphs
G_{i}
. By a
face
of
G
we mean the closure of a connected component of the complement
C\setminus G
.

We first give a general description of the flag moduli space for
C
not necessarily simply connected. We will not use this in our applications, so the reader is welcome to skip to the simpler Definition
5.2
, which is equivalent when
C
is simply connected.

Let
\Sigma(G)\subset C\times\mathbb{R}
be the wavefront of the Legendrian weave, woven according to
G\subseteq C
. Call a
region
a connected component of the complement
(C\times\mathbb{R})\setminus\Sigma(G).

Definition 5.1
.

Let
C
be a connected surface and let
G\subseteq C
be an
N
-graph. The
framed flag moduli space

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(C,G)
associated to
G
is comprised of the following data.

i)

A rank-
N
local system
E\longrightarrow C
, equivalently a vector space
V
and a representation of the based fundamental group
\pi_{1}(C)
on
V
.

ii)

For each face
F
of the
N
-graph
G
, a compatible local system of flags
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}(F)
.

iii)

For each pair of adjacent faces
F_{1},F_{2}
, sharing an
i
-edge
e
, their two associated compatible local systems of flags
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}(F_{1}),\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}(F_{2})
are in relative position
\tau_{i}\in S_{N}
, and along the common edge
e
we have chosen isomorphisms

\mathscr{F}^{j}(F_{1})\cong\mathscr{F}^{j}(F_{2}),\quad 0\leq j\leq N,j\neq i,

and no other information, as
\mathscr{F}^{i}(F_{1})\ncong\mathscr{F}^{i}(F_{2})
.

iv)

By gluing, these isomorphisms define local systems in each region, since the
j
th step of a flag of local systems
\mathscr{F}^{j}
compatible with
E
defines a local system on the
region between the
j
th and
(j+1)
st sheets — and these are not separated by a
\tau_{i}
crossing of sheets when
j\neq i
. We require that such local systems in regions, each of which are sub-local systems of
E
via upward generization morphisms, are compatible with
E
.
18
18

18

This condition is not local in the
N
-graph,
G
.

The group
\operatorname{PGL}_{N}
acts on the space
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(C,G)
diagonally, i.e. as isomorphisms of
E
and on all flags of local systems at once. By definition, the
flag moduli space
of the
N
-graph
G
is the quotient stack

\mathcal{M}(C,G):=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(C,G)/\operatorname{PGL}_{N}.

We simply write
\mathcal{M}(G)
when
C
is understood.

\Box

Definition 5.2
.

Let
C
be a connected, simply connected surface and let
G\subseteq C
be an
N
-graph. The
framed flag moduli space

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(C,G)
associated to
G
is comprised of tuples of flags, specifically:

i)

There is a flag
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}(F)
assigned to each face
F
of the
N
-graph
G
.

ii)

For each pair of adjacent faces
F_{1},F_{2}\subseteq C\setminus G
, sharing an
i
-edge, their two associated flags
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}(F_{1}),\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}(F_{2})
are in relative position
\tau_{i}\in S_{N}
, i.e. they must satisfy

\mathscr{F}^{j}(F_{1})=\mathscr{F}^{j}(F_{2}),\quad 0\leq j\leq N,j\neq i,\mbox{ and }\mathscr{F}^{i}(F_{1})\neq\mathscr{F}^{i}(F_{2}).

The group
\operatorname{GL}_{N}
acts on the space
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(C,G)
diagonally, i.e. on all flags at once. By definition, the
flag moduli space
of the
N
-graph
G
is the quotient stack

\mathcal{M}(C,G):=\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(C,G)/\operatorname{PGL}_{N}.

We simply write
\mathcal{M}(G)
when
C
is understood.

\Box

We will equivalently exchange between the linear and projective perspective for a full flag. In the projective setting, flags
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}
(or local systems of flags) are understood as a sequence of nested projective planes
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{F})^{\bullet}
, given by the projectivization of the linear spaces of the linear flag
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}
. For a ground field
R
, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(C,G;R)
is representable by an Artin stack of finite type
[
LO08
,
LO09
]
, and is typically an algebraic variety (unless
G
is so symmetric that an admissible configuration of flags might be fixed by
\operatorname{PGL}_{N}
).

In Subsection
5.3
we explain why the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(C,G;R)
is an invariant of the Legendrian isotopy type of the associated Legendrian weave
\Lambda(G)\subseteq(J^{1}C,\xi_{\text{st}})
. The algebraic questions we are interested in this article are about the different properties and computations of the moduli
\mathcal{M}(C,G;R)
— for instance the cardinality of
|\mathcal{M}(C,G;\mathbb{F}_{q})|
over a finite field or how
\mathcal{M}(C,G;R)
changes upon performing the combinatorial moves in Section
4
, including Legendrian mutations and surgeries. To ease notation, we will denote flags
\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}
by
\mathscr{F}
.

5.3.
Sheaf Description of Flag Moduli and Invariance

Let
C
be a smooth surface,
R
a commutative ring, and
\operatorname{Sh}(C\times\mathbb{R})
the category of
constructible sheaves,
i.e. the
R
-linear dg-derived category of complexes of sheaves of
R
-modules on
C\times\mathbb{R}
with constructible cohomology sheaves. For algebraic preliminaries on (derived) dg-categories we refer the reader to
[
Kel94
,
Tab05
,
Toe07
,
LO10
]
, and for simplicity we will choose
R
a field. In this section, we use the identification
J^{1}(C)\cong T^{\infty,-}(C\times\mathbb{R})
of the first jet bundle of
C
with downward covectors of
C\times\mathbb{R}
— see
[
NRS+15
, Section 2.1]
. Now given an
N
-graph
G\subseteq C
,
the Legendrian
\Lambda(G)\subset J^{1}(C)\cong T^{\infty,-}(C\times\mathbb{R})\subset T^{\infty}(C\times\mathbb{R})
can
be used to define the subcategory
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda(G)}(C\times\mathbb{R})\subset\operatorname{Sh}(C\times\mathbb{R})
whose objects are constructible sheaves whose singular support at contact infinity is contained in
\Lambda(G)\subset T^{\infty}(C\times\mathbb{R})
— see
[
TZ18
, Section 4]
.

We write
\mathcal{C}(C,G):=\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\Lambda(G)}(C\times\mathbb{R})_{0}\subset\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda(G)}(C\times\mathbb{R})
for the subategory of microlocal
rank-one sheaves which are zero in a neighborhood of
C\times\{-\infty\},
or
\mathcal{C}(G)
for short. This has a simple description, which we now explain.
The dg-category
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda(G)}(C\times\mathbb{R})
is itself a subcategory of sheaves constructible with respect to the stratification defined
by the front projection
\Sigma(G)
, and thus has a combinatorial description.
By
[
KS90
, Theorem 8.1.11]
, it is equivalent to the dg-category of functors from the poset of strata to
k
-mod (chain complexes) — see also

[
Nad09
, Section 2.3]
and
[
STZ17
, Section 3.3]
.
The subcategory cut out by
\mathcal{C}(G)
is the one whose objects are isomorphic to ones with the following properties: the chain complex assigned to a neighborhood of
C\times\{-\infty\}
is zero; the complexes in each region of
(C\times\mathbb{R})\setminus\Sigma(G)
are rank-one local systems (or just vector spaces if
C
is simply connected); the morphisms assigned to all downward restriction maps are isomorphisms; and the upward morphisms from small open sets intersecting
\Sigma(G)
to the regions above them which do not are codimension-one inclusions.

The combinatorial model for this description leads to the flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(G)
of isomorphism classes of objects in
\mathcal{C}(G).
Indeed, the flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(G)
associated to an
N
-graph
G\subseteq C
, as introduced in Definition
5.2
, relates to the category
\mathcal{C}(G):=\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\Lambda(G)}(C\times\mathbb{R})_{0}
according to the following result, which itself generalizes
[
TZ18
, Section 4.3]
to
N
-graphs with
N\geq 3
:

Theorem 5.3
.

The flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(C,G;R)
is isomorphic to the moduli space of objects in
\mathcal{C}(G):=\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\Lambda(G)}(C\times\mathbb{R})_{0}
, the subcategory of microlocal rank-
1
objects in
\operatorname{Sh}_{\Lambda(G)}(C\times\mathbb{R})
supported away from
C\times\{-\infty\}.

Proof.

We first assume that
C
is simply connected. The argument parallels that of
[
STZ17
, Sections 6.2 and 6.3]
, with the additions required by the strictly two-dimensional behavior. The moduli space of objects is defined locally, meaning that it is the fiber product over its restriction-to-boundary maps of the moduli spaces
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
in neighborhoods of
C
. We can assume that these neighborhoods of
C
are chosen small enough so that they are contractible and contain no more than one “feature” of the given
N
-graph
G
. That is, for some such neighborhood
U\subseteq C
, either
U\cap G
is empty or contains part of an edge, a single trivalent vertex, or a single hexagonal vertex. We then have a local study for each of these cases.

In the case where
U
is empty or contains part of an edge, the front of the Legendrian weave over
U
is either
N
parallel sheets or
N
sheets with a single crossing labeled
\tau_{i}
, and can be identified with
\sigma\times\mathbb{R}
, where
\sigma
is a front of a one-dimensional Legendrian knot being either
N
parallel lines or
N
lines with a single crossing. Then, since the
\mathbb{R}
factor is contractible, we can identify the moduli space
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
over
U
using the one-dimensional study in
[
STZ17
, Sections 6.2 and 6.3]
, concluding that it is either the flag variety or pairs of
\tau_{i}
-transverse flags, respectively.

The moduli
\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\Lambda(G)}(C\times\mathbb{R})
is local with respect to
G\subseteq C
and the topology of the surface
C
, i.e. it is globally described as fibered products for the local pieces of
G\subseteq C
.
It therefore remains to show that
\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\Lambda(G)}(C\times\mathbb{R})
coincides with
\mathcal{M}(C,G)
for the local graphs
G_{tri}\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{2}
and
G_{hex}\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{2}
, respectively given by a trivalent vertex and a hexagonal vertex, as introduced in Section
2
. We do these in turn.

The trivalent vertex case was studied in
[
TZ18
, Section 4]
for
2
-graphs, and we will make the needed adjustments to
N
-graphs.
The computation for the local
N
-graph
G_{tri}
consists of an analysis of the moduli of constructible sheaves supported at the
D_{4}^{-}
-wavefront singularity, as directly carried out in
[
TZ18
]
. The boundary conditions for an object in
\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\Lambda(G_{tri})}(\mathbb{D}^{2}\times\mathbb{R})
consist of a triple of flags
(\mathscr{F}_{1},\mathscr{F}_{2},\mathscr{F}_{3})
such that
\mathscr{F}_{i}\in S_{\tau_{k}}(\mathscr{F}_{j})
for
i\neq j
,
1\leq i,j\leq 3
, if the edges of
G_{tri}
are labeled by
\tau_{k}
. This can be seen by combining the result for a neighborhood of a single crossing edge above, taking the fiber product over the spaces of flags in the empty neighborhoods in-between. Then
[
TZ18
, Section 4.1]
implies that these are all the required conditions (and strata) and thus
\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\Lambda(G_{tri})}(\mathbb{D}^{2}\times\mathbb{R})
coincides with
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{D}^{2},G_{tri})
. Note that the analysis in
[
TZ18
, Subsection 4.1.2]
restricts to the case where the local model is a 2-graph
G_{tri}\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{2}
, it is readily seen that this model suffices for the analysis of the local model
N
-graph
G_{tri}\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{2}
.

Figure 75.
Constructible sheaf in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
microlocally supported on the
A_{3}
-swallowtail singularity (left). The sheaf convolution given by the Guillermou-Kashiwara-Schapira
[
GKS12
]
quantization upon performing a Reidemeister R1 move (right).

Alternatively, it is possible to directly conclude the analysis of the
D_{4}^{-}
-singularity by performing a generic perturbation of the
D_{4}^{-}
-wavefront, as depicted in Figure
36
, and studying the category of constructible sheaves supported at a
A_{3}
-swallowtail singularity. Indeed, Figure
75
(left) shows the conditions for a constructible sheaf microlocally supported along the front of an
A_{3}
-swallowtail singularity, which consists of a choice of injective map
f:C_{1}\longrightarrow C_{2}
, where
C_{1}\cong R^{k}
and
C_{1}\cong R^{k+1}
, for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. The crucial fact is that the (stalk of the) sheaf in the remaining 3-dimensional open strata
I
is uniquely determined to be the cone of the map
(f,-f):C_{1}\longrightarrow C_{2}\oplus C_{2}
. This is a consequence of the Guillermou-Kashiwara-Schapira quantization
[
GKS12
, Theorem 3.7]
of Legendrian isotopies: since the
A_{3}
-swallowtail is the big wavefront
[
Ad90
]
of the first Reidemeister move for 1-dimensional Legendrian fronts, it follows that the sheaves in the strata
I
are uniquely determined by
f:C_{1}\longrightarrow C_{2}
by the sheaf kernel associated to the first Reidemeister move. It is readily seen
[
STZ17
]
that the result of the convolution with such a kernel yields the sheaf transformation in Figure
75
(right). By the non-characteristic property of the category of microlocal sheaves
[
GKS12
]
, the sheaves microlocally supported on the wavefront of the
D_{4}^{-}
-singularity is equivalent to that for a generic perturbation of such
D_{4}^{-}
-singularity. The generic perturbation consists of three
A_{3}
-swallowtails and the conditions for the constructible sheaves on these stratification follow from the above analysis. In conclusion, we obtain an isomorphism
\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\Lambda(G_{tri})}(\mathbb{D}^{2}\times\mathbb{R})\cong\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{D}^{2},G_{tri})
.

Let us now address the hexagonal vertex
G_{hex}
. Since the Legendrian weave
\Sigma(G_{hex})
is the big wavefront of the third Reidemeister move for 1-dimensional Legendrian fronts, it suffices to understand the kernel of its quantization. Figure
76
shows the local transformation for constructible sheaves near the third Reidemeister move
[
STZ17
, Section 4.4.3]
.

Figure 76.
The explicit flag exchange given by the Guillermou-Kashiwara-Schapira
[
GKS12
]
quantization upon performing a Reidemeister R3 move.

In Figure
76
, the
C_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq 5
and
E_{1},E_{2}
are complexes of vectors spaces, which we can actually assume to be vector spaces
[
STZ17
, Section 3.3]
. If
C_{1}\cong R^{k}
, for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the microlocal rank 1 condition implies that
E_{1},E_{2},C_{2},C_{3}\cong R^{k+1}
,
C_{4},C_{5}\cong R^{k+2}
and
C_{6}\cong R^{k+3}
. The four flags at one of the sides of the hexagonal vertex are

\mathscr{F}^{(1)}_{1}=C_{1}\longrightarrow C_{3}\longrightarrow C_{5}\longrightarrow C_{6},\quad\mathscr{F}^{(1)}_{2}=C_{1}\longrightarrow E_{1}\longrightarrow C_{5}\longrightarrow C_{6},

\mathscr{F}^{(1)}_{3}=C_{1}\longrightarrow E_{1}\longrightarrow C_{4}\longrightarrow C_{6},\quad\mathscr{F}^{(1)}_{4}=C_{1}\longrightarrow C_{2}\longrightarrow C_{4}\longrightarrow C_{6},

and the four flags at the other side of the hexagonal vertex are

\mathscr{F}^{(2)}_{1}=C_{1}\longrightarrow C_{3}\longrightarrow C_{5}\longrightarrow C_{6},\quad\mathscr{F}^{(2)}_{2}=C_{1}\longrightarrow C_{3}\longrightarrow E_{2}\longrightarrow C_{6},

\mathscr{F}^{(2)}_{3}=C_{1}\longrightarrow C_{2}\longrightarrow E_{2}\longrightarrow C_{6},\quad\mathscr{F}^{(2)}_{4}=C_{1}\longrightarrow C_{2}\longrightarrow C_{4}\longrightarrow C_{6}.

The three crossings in Figure
76
(left) imply, from left to right, that

\mathscr{F}^{(1)}_{1}\in S_{\tau_{k+1}}(\mathscr{F}^{(1)}_{2}),\mathscr{F}^{(1)}_{2}\in S_{\tau_{k+2}}(\mathscr{F}^{(1)}_{3}),\mathscr{F}^{(1)}_{3}\in S_{\tau_{k+1}}(\mathscr{F}^{(1)}_{4}).

Similarly, the three crossings in Figure
76
(right) imply, from left to right, that

\mathscr{F}^{(2)}_{1}\in S_{\tau_{k+2}}(\mathscr{F}^{(2)}_{2}),\mathscr{F}^{(2)}_{2}\in S_{\tau_{k+1}}(\mathscr{F}^{(2)}_{3}),\mathscr{F}^{(2)}_{3}\in S_{\tau_{k+2}}(\mathscr{F}^{(2)}_{4}).

These are precisely the conditions for the flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{D}^{2},G_{hex})
in Definition
5.2
, and hence
\operatorname{Sh}^{1}_{\Lambda(G_{hex})}(\mathbb{D}^{2}\times\mathbb{R})\cong\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{D}^{2},G_{hex})
.

This concludes the argument for the case where
C
is simply connected.
We now turn to the case where
C
is not simply connected. There are no further local conditions. The only additional concerns regard compatibilities of local systems.

Let
\Sigma(G)\subset C\times\mathbb{R}
be the wavefront of the Legendrian weave, and recall that we call a
region
a connected component of the complement
(C\times\mathbb{R})\setminus\Sigma(G).
A constructible sheaf in
\mathcal{C}(G)
restricts to a local system on each region, since there is no singular support away from the wavefront. There are two distinguished regions
R_{\rm top}
and
R_{\rm bot}
containing neighborhoods of
C\times\{\infty\}
and
C\times\{-\infty\}
, respectively. A constructible sheaf in
\mathcal{C}(G)
restricts to
0
in
R_{\rm bot}
(by definition) and to a local system on
R_{\rm top}\sim C
that we assign to be the data
E
from Definition
5.1
(i).
Now, as explained in Definition
5.1
(iv), the data of a point in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(G)
defines a local system in each region.
Commutativity of sheaf restriction maps requires that a section which is parallel transported around a region and then included into
E
arrives at the same place as a section which is included first and then parallel transported around
R_{\rm top},
and this is the requirement of Definition
5.1
(iv). ∎

5.4.
Local Flag Moduli Computations

Let us prove the following useful lemmas on the flag moduli, which can be implicitly used when performing computations on
\mathcal{M}(C,G;R)
. In this section, and subsequent computations, we will consider a ground field
R=k
, with
k=\mathbb{C}
and finite fields
k=\mathbb{F}_{q}
as the main fields of interest.

We start with the study of the flag moduli space at a trivalent vertex, as depicted in the left of Figure
8
, and characterize that local flag moduli space.

Lemma 5.4
.

Consider the neighborhood
{\mathcal{O}}{\it p}_{3}(N)
of a
\tau_{i}
-trivalent vertex in an
N
-graph. Then the local moduli of flags
\mathcal{M}({\mathcal{O}}{\it p}_{3},G;k)
is set-theoretically a point, and the
\operatorname{PGL}_{N}
-action on
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{O}}{\it p}_{3},G;k)
has stabilizer
(k^{*})^{N-2}\times k^{{N\choose 2}-1}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{F}_{1},\mathscr{F}_{2},\mathscr{F}_{3}
be the three flags in
{\mathcal{O}}{\it p}_{3}(N)
. The
\operatorname{GL}_{N}
-action is transitive on the space of flags, and thus
\mathscr{F}_{1}
can be mapped to the standard flag
\mathscr{S}_{1}
, defined by

\mathscr{S}^{j}_{1}=\{x_{j+1}=x_{j+2}=\ldots=x_{N-1}=x_{N}=0\},\mbox{ where }k=\operatorname{Spec}k[x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}].

The
\operatorname{GL}_{N}
-action allows us to also map the two flags
\mathscr{F}_{2}
and
\mathscr{F}_{3}
, respectively, to
\mathscr{S}_{2}
and
\mathscr{S}_{3}
, defined by

\mathscr{S}^{j}_{2}=\mathscr{S}^{j}_{3}=\mathscr{S}^{j}_{1},\quad 0\leq j\leq N,\quad j\neq i,

\mathscr{S}_{2}^{j}=\{x_{i}=x_{i+2}=\ldots=x_{N-1}=x_{N}=0\},

\mathscr{S}_{3}^{j}=\{x_{i}-x_{i+1}=x_{i+2}=\ldots=x_{N-1}=x_{N}=0\}.

This implies that the quotient of the moduli
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{O}}{\it p}_{3},G;R)
by the gauge group
\operatorname{PGL}_{N}
is set-theoretically a point. In order to recover its structure as a quotient stack, it suffices to identify the stabilizer of the triple of flags
\mathscr{S}_{1},\mathscr{S}_{2},\mathscr{S}_{3}
. For that, notice that the stabilizer of
\mathscr{S}_{1}
is the projectivization of the Borel subgroup of upper triangular matrices, isomorphic to
(k^{*})^{N-1}\times k^{{N\choose 2}}
. The condition of fixing the flag
\mathscr{S}_{2}
transversely cuts out a
k
-coordinate in the interior of the upper triangle, since it sets the
(i,i+1)
entry equals to zero. This cuts the stabilizer down to
(k^{*})^{N-1}\times k^{{N\choose 2}-1}
, and finally stabilizing
\mathscr{S}_{3}
imposes the equality of the two diagonal entries
(i,i)
and
(i+1,i+1)
, thus transversely cutting down a
k^{*}
. The resulting stabilizer is
(k^{*})^{N-2}\times k^{{N\choose 2}-1}
, as claimed.
∎

In its simplest instance of
N=2
, this is the statement that three distinct points in the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}(k)
can be sent to
\{0,1,\infty\}
with trivial stabilizer. A lesson from Lemma
5.4
is that for any
N
, near at least one trivalent vertex of an
N
-graph, we are allowed to use the gauge group
\operatorname{PGL}_{N}
and fix the flags around that vertex. The (proof of the) lemma also provides the (geometric) degrees of freedom left after this choice.

Example 5.5
.

Consider the
2
-graph
G
associated to the triangulation of
C=S^{2}
with two triangles. This
2
-graph
G
, dual to the triangulation, has two vertices, three edges and three faces. Then the flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{S}^{2},G;\mathbb{C})
consists of a point
\{*\}
. In fact, this point
\{*\}
of the flag moduli space geometrically corresponds to the conjecturally unique Lagrangian 3-disk filling of the standard Legendrian unknot
\Lambda_{0}\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
.
\Box

Lemma
5.4
is a statement about a particular triple of flags. It ought to be noted that a
generic
triple of flags is part of a moduli space of dimension
{N-1\choose 2}
, with birational coordinates given by generalized triple ratios – see Section
7
and
[
FG06b
, Section 9]
. The flags appearing in the context of our
N
-graphs are in general a combination of non-generic flags, arising from the local vertices, with a flag being modified at exactly one degree when crossing an edge.

Let us now address our second local model at a vertex, that of a hexagonal vertex, as depicted in the right of Figure
8
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Consider the neighborhood
{\mathcal{O}}{\it p}_{6}(N)
of a hexagonal vertex, with edges
\tau_{i}
,
\tau_{i+1}
and consecutively ordered flags
\mathscr{F}_{j}
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}
. Then any pair of opposite flags
\mathscr{F}_{k},\mathscr{F}_{k+3}
determines the others.

Proof.

By symmetry, it suffices to show that the flags
\mathscr{F}_{1},\mathscr{F}_{4}
determine
\mathscr{F}_{5}
and
\mathscr{F}_{6}
. We assume that
\mathscr{F}_{4}
and
\mathscr{F}_{5}
are separated by a
\tau_{i+1}
edge — a similar argument will work if it is of type
\tau_{i}
.
By the prescribed transversality, we have
\mathscr{F}^{j}_{6}=\mathscr{F}^{j}_{1}
and
\mathscr{F}^{j}_{5}=\mathscr{F}^{j}_{4}
for
j\neq i+1
.
Now since
\mathscr{F}_{5}^{i}\neq\mathscr{F}_{6}^{i}
, and
\mathscr{F}_{1}^{i+2}=\mathscr{F}_{4}^{i+2}
, there exists a unique linear subspace
V\subseteq\mathscr{F}_{1}^{i+2}
which contains
\mathscr{F}_{5}^{i},\mathscr{F}_{6}^{i}
. So we must have
\mathscr{F}^{i+1}_{5}=\mathscr{F}^{i+1}_{6}=V
, uniquely determining the flags
\mathscr{F}_{5}
and
\mathscr{F}_{6}
.
∎

A direct application of Lemma
5.6
is the invariance of the moduli of flags under the
N
-graph Reidemeister Move I from our Theorem
4.2
above:

Corollary 5.7
.

The flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(C,G;R)
is invariant under the candy twist.
\Box

The candy twist – Move I – is the move depicted in Figure
19
above, and the proof of Corollary
5.7
follows immediately from Lemma
5.6
, since the interior faces of the local model are uniquely determined by two opposing boundary flags, and they in turn determine the remaining ones. Corollary
5.7
also follows from Theorem
1.1
and the Legendrian invariance proven in
[
GKS12
, Theorem 3.7]
. The invariance of the moduli of flags under the other moves in Theorem
4.2
can be proven similarly by direct means.

Lemma
5.6
discusses the flags in a neighborhood of a hexagonal vertex and allows for a computation of the local flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}({\mathcal{O}}{\it p}_{6}(N);R)
at a hexagonal vertex, since it reduces it to the study of a quadruple of flags.

Example 5.8
.

Let us illustrate this point by computing
\mathcal{M}({\mathcal{O}}{\it p}_{6}(3);\mathbb{C})
, which we claim is isomorphic to a point stabilized by the subgroup
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}\subseteq\operatorname{PGL}(3,\mathbb{C})
. Indeed, the incidence problem at a hexagonal vertex is given by six flags

\mathscr{F}_{1}=(p_{1},l_{1}),\quad\mathscr{F}_{2}=(p_{2},l_{1}),\quad\mathscr{F}_{3}=(p_{2},l_{2}),

\mathscr{F}_{4}=(p_{3},l_{2}),\quad\mathscr{F}_{5}=(p_{3},l_{3}),\quad\mathscr{F}_{6}=(p_{1},l_{3})

where
p_{i}
and
l_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq 4
, are points and lines in
\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{C})
and the notation
(p_{i},l_{i})
stands for the projectivized flag
p_{i}\in l_{i}
. Since the three points
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}
are pairwise distinct,
\operatorname{PGL}(3,\mathbb{C})
acts on them transitively, and their stabilizer is the (projectivization) of a maximal torus in
\operatorname{GL}(3,\mathbb{C})
, which is isomorphic to
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}
. Lemma
5.6
provides a more direct route: it suffices to observe that the
\operatorname{PGL}(3,\mathbb{C})
-stabilizer of the two completely transverse flags
\mathscr{F}_{1},\mathscr{F}_{4}
is the set of diagonal matrices in
\operatorname{PGL}(3,\mathbb{C})
, i.e.
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}
.
\Box

It is an exercise to extend the argument for Lemma
5.4
above in this context and show that:

Lemma 5.9
.

Consider the neighborhood
{\mathcal{O}}{\it p}_{6}(N)
of a
(\tau_{i},\tau_{i+1})
-hexagonal vertex in an
N
-graph. Then the local moduli of flags
\mathcal{M}({\mathcal{O}}{\it p}_{6},G;k)
is set-theoretically a point, and the
\operatorname{PGL}_{N}
-action on
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{O}}{\it p}_{6},G;k)
has stabilizer
(k^{*})^{2}\times\left((k^{*})^{N-3}\times k^{{N\choose 2}-3}\right)
.
\Box

Having computed the local models at trivalent and hexagonal vertices, in Lemmas
5.4
and
5.9
, we now address the local flag moduli space around a
\tau_{i}
-edge connecting two trivalent vertices for
1\leq i\leq N-1
. Thanks to our discussion in Subsection
2.4
on the homology of the associated Legendrian weaves, we know that this is the flag moduli space associated to a Legendrian cylinder. In contrast to Lemmas
5.4
and
5.9
above, we will now discover that the local flag moduli space around a monochromatic edge is (set-theoretically) non-trivial.

Lemma 5.10

(Flag Cross-ratio)
.

Let
G
be an
N
-graph, and
e\in G
a monochromatic edge between two trivalent vertices. The local flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}({\mathcal{O}}{\it p}(e),G;k)
in a neighborhood
{\mathcal{O}}{\it p}(e)
is isomorphic to
k^{*}
with stabilizer
(k^{*})^{N-2}\times k^{{N\choose 2}-1}
, under the
\operatorname{PGL}_{N}
-action.

Lemma
5.10
appears in the study of cluster coordinates for
2
-graphs in the works
[
FG06b
,
TZ18
]
, yet a treatment of it here, in the context of
N
-graphs, seems in order. The interesting part in Lemma
5.10
is the existence of a non-trivial flag moduli space around the edge
e\in G
. The stabilizer only appears due to the dependence on
N
. Note also that, by using Lemma
5.6
, the statement in Lemma
5.10
can readily be generalized for a long edge
e
, i.e. an
\sf I
-cycle between two trivalent vertices, as described in Section
2
.

Proof.

For an edge
e\in G
between two trivalent vertices, it suffices to discuss the case of a monochromatic edge, since the push-through move preserves the flag moduli. In this case, let
v_{1},v_{2}
be the two endpoints of
e
. By Lemma
5.4
, the local flag moduli space around
v_{1}
can be fixed to be a point with stabilizer
(k^{*})^{N-2}\times k^{{N\choose 2}-1}
. In this normalization, the flag moduli space around
v_{2}
is determined in two of the sectors, and thus it is uniquely described by the remaining choice of flag. This is tantamount to the choice of a fourth point in
\mathbb{P}^{1}(k)\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
, which yields a modulus of
k^{*}
.
∎

In general, the existence of a non-trivial
1
-cycle
\gamma\in H_{1}(\Lambda(G),\mathbb{Z})
provides the flag moduli space with a
k^{*}
factor, which can be geometrically interpreted as being a contribution of the microlocal monodromy of the associated local system induced in the Legendrian surface
\Lambda(G)
, as we explain in Section
7
. The following example illustrates this point in the case of a
\sf Y
-cycle in
G
.

Example 5.11
.

Let us compute the local flag moduli space in an
N=3
neighborhood of a
\sf Y
-cycle, as depicted in Figure
11
(Right). The configurations of points for this incidence problem are given by the following conditions:

(a)

Three distinct points
p_{0},p_{1},p_{2}
, and three points
q_{i}\in l_{i}=\langle p_{i},p_{i+1}\rangle
, where the index
0\leq i\leq 2
is understood modulo
3
,

(b)

The triples
\{p_{i},p_{i+1},q_{i}\}
,
0\leq i\leq 2
, are triples of distinct points.

The action of
\operatorname{PGL}_{3}
allows us to set
p_{0}=[1:0:0]
,
p_{1}=[0:1:0]
and
p_{2}=[0:0:1]
with a
(k^{*})^{2}
Cartan stabilizer, and this stabilizer can then be used to fix
q_{0}=[1:1:0]\in l_{0}
and
q_{1}=[0:1:1]
. The remaining choice of
q_{2}
yields the
k^{*}
contribution to the flag moduli space since it is a choice of a point
q_{2}\in l_{2}
distinct from
p_{1},p_{2}
.
\Box

This concludes our local computations of flag moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(C,G)
. We now study the behavior of the invariant
\mathcal{M}(C,G)
under Legendrian surgery, and Sections
6
and
8
will develop global computation of flag moduli spaces. Given an
N
-graph
G\subseteq C
, we ease notation by writing
\mathcal{M}(G)
for
\mathcal{M}(C,G)
.

5.5.
Flag Moduli under Legendrian Surgeries

Let
G,G^{\prime}
be
N
-graphs such that
G^{\prime}
is obtained by Legendrian surgery on
G
, as described in Theorem
4.10
. The following result relates the flag moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(G)
and
\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime})
before and after Legendrian surgery.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
k
be a field and
G
an
N
-graph. For any
\tau_{i}
-edges of
G
, the flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(G)
satisfies the following local relations:

Figure 77
.
The change of the flag moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(G)
under combinatorial changes in a piece of an
N
-graph
G
.

Proof.

The relations can be verified with our description of the flag moduli space in Subsection
5.2
. We can also argue directly thanks to the geometry developed in Section
4
. Indeed, the moduli of objects of the category of constructible sheaves microlocally supported at a Legendrian connected sum
\Lambda_{1}\#\Lambda_{2}
is a direct product of the moduli of objects microlocally supported at
\Lambda_{1}
and those microlocally supported at
\Lambda_{2}
. By Theorem
4.10
, the right and left graphs
G_{r},G_{l}
for the Relations
(i)
and
(ii)
geometrically correspond to Legendrian connected sums with the standard Legendrian 2-torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{\text{st}}
, and the Legendrian Clifford 2-torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{c}
, respectively. The flag moduli for the former is
k^{*}
, and for the latter it is
k\setminus\{0,1\}
, which concludes
(i),(ii)
. Finally, the relation
(iii)
follows from Proposition
4.24
, as there do not exist constructible sheaves microlocally supported at a loose Legendrian.
∎

Note that, by construction, there exists a 3-dimensional exact Lagrangian cobordism
L(G,G^{\prime})
from
\Lambda(G)
to
\Lambda(G^{\prime})
, in the symplectization of
(J^{1}C,\xi_{\text{st}})
. Thus, from the standard results in Floer theory
[
EES05b
,
EGH00
]
, we expect
19
19

19

To our knowledge, these maps have yet to be studied in the context of microlocal sheaf theory. The expectation that they exist comes from the fact that the flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(G)
should correspond to an augmentation variety for
\Lambda(G)
, and these maps are known to exist between augmentation varieties.
a map from
\mathcal{M}(G)\times H_{1}(L(G,G^{\prime}),k)
to
\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime})
. Theorem
5.12
gives a strong indication of what these maps should be, i.e. for
(i),(ii)
,
\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime})
is a
k^{*}
- or a
(k\setminus\{0,1\})
-bundle over
\mathcal{M}(G)
, with the map being a section for this bundle projection.

5.6.
Non-characterstic Property of Stabilization

We conclude Section
5
with an interesting and direct computation of flag moduli spaces. First, note that the proof of Theorem
4.17
, showing that the standard satellites of
\Lambda(G)
and
\Lambda(s(G))
are Legendrian isotopic, and Theorem
5.3
imply the isomorphism

\mathcal{M}(\Lambda(G))\cong\mathcal{M}(\Lambda(s(G))),

where
s(G)
is the stabilization we introduced in Subsection
4.7
. We will nevertheless provide a self-contained sheaf-theoretical proof of that equivalence, which we now illustrate in the case
N=2
.

Proof of flag moduli space equivalence
N=2
.

In that case, the moduli of objects in the category
\mathcal{M}(\Lambda(s(G)))
parametrizes flags in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
up to
\operatorname{PGL}(3,\mathbb{C})
equivalence abiding the constraints imposed by the 3-graph on the left of Figure
78
. We assume that the 2-graph
G=G^{1,2}
, before stabilizing, contains at least a vertex.

Figure 78.
The flag configuration for
N=2
stabilization.

The graph
G^{(1,2)}
imposes constraints on the points lying in a line
l_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
, the ladybug changes this line to distinct lines
l_{2},l_{3}
, also different from
l_{1}
, and the descending
(12)
-halo provides the freedom of a point
p\in l_{3}
. The fact that
G^{(1,2)}
is contained in a wing of the ladybug implies that
l_{1}\cap l_{2}\cap l_{3}
is a point, which for now we denote
\infty
. Let us show that this moduli space coincides with the moduli space of points in
l_{1}
imposed by
G^{(1,2)}
. For that, note that the stabilizer of three non-collinear points
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
; indeed, it is isomorphic to the space of invertible diagonal matrices in
\operatorname{PGL}(3,\mathbb{C})
. Geometrically, each of the
\mathbb{C}^{*}
allows us to move any point in one of the three possible lines spanned by two of the three points
\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}
around that line, on the complement of these two spanning points.

Hence we can start by using the
\operatorname{PGL}(3,\mathbb{C})
and fix the points
1,p,\infty\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
in the configuration shown in the right of Figure
78
, which determine the lines
l_{1},l_{3}
. From the
\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
we can use the first
\mathbb{C}^{*}
in order to send the third point in
l_{1}
imposed by
G^{(12)}
to
0\in l_{1}
, and the second
\mathbb{C}^{*}
to choose a point in the line
l=\langle 1,p\rangle
, which in turn determines a line
l_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
by taking its span with
\infty\in l_{1}\cap l_{3}
. This fixes the configuration of lines
l_{1},l_{2},l_{3}
and the points
{0,1,\infty,p}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
with
\{0,1,\infty\}\subseteq l_{1}
, and that is precisely the three points being fixed by the
\operatorname{PGL}(2,\mathbb{C})
symmetry acting in
G^{(12)}
.
∎

This argument is self-contained, yet hopefully illustrates how in general the
geometric
conclusion from Theorem
4.17
, and the invariance of the flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}
under Legendrian isotopy, are stronger and neater tools than the strict algebraic invariance of the flag moduli space. Let us now move forward with the following Sections
6
,
7
and
8
, which display several applications of the techniques developed in Sections
2
,
3
,
4
and
5
, and in particular prove Theorems
1.5
,
1.6
,
1.8
and
1.9
stated in the introduction.

6.
Applications and Vexillary Computations

In this section we study applications of our diagrammatic calculus for Legendrian weaves
\Lambda(G)
associated to an
N
-graph
G
, and their flag moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(G)
. In particular, we will prove Theorem
1.5
and Theorem
1.6
.

6.1.
First Pair of Computations

Let us start with two simple examples of Legendrian weaves and their flag moduli: the Legendrian Clifford torus and the double
t_{4}\cup t_{4}\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{2}
of the 4-triangle
t_{4}
in the 2-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

6.1.1.
The Legendrian Clifford Torus

Let us consider the 2-graph
G=(\partial\Delta^{3})^{(1)}\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{2}
in Figure
79
, which has already featured in the proof of Theorem
4.10
. The flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(G)
is readily seen to be the pair of pants
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
. Indeed, there are four contractible connected components in
\mathbb{S}^{2}\setminus G
, which implies that

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(G)=\{(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})\in(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{4}:p_{i}\neq p_{j},\quad i\neq j\}

where
\mathbb{P}^{1}\cong\operatorname{GL}(2,\mathbb{C})/B
is the flag variety of lines in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Since
\operatorname{PGL}(2,\mathbb{C})
acts 3-transitively on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we can assume that
(p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=(0,1,\infty)
, and the quotient
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(G)/PGL(3,\mathbb{C})
is given by

\mathcal{M}(G)=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{P}^{1}:\lambda\neq 0,1,\infty\}.

This flag moduli space is shown in Figure
79
(left), which is uniquely determined by the choice of
\lambda\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
.

Figure 79.
The tetrahedral 2-graph
G
as a planar projection of the 1-skeleton
(\Delta^{3})^{(1)}
of the tetrahedron
\partial\Delta
(left). A front projection for the Legendrian 2-torus
\iota(\Lambda(G))
(right).

Let us illustrate the Legendrian geometry in this case. The Euler characteristic of the Legendrian weave
\Lambda(G)
is
\chi(\Lambda(G))=2\cdot\chi(\mathbb{S}^{2})-4=0
, and thus
\Lambda(G)
is a closed 2-torus. A different front for
\Lambda(G)
is depicted in Figure
79
(right), where the cone singularity
[
CM19
, Section 2]
is used, in line with the description in
[
DR11
, Section 3]
. The flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(G)
for the 2-graph
G
is read in this front as the moduli space of constructible sheaves in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
microlocally supported with rank-1 in the front Figure
79
(right). This latter moduli is given with the data of a 1-dimensional vector space
\mathbb{C}
in the bounded region in the interior of the front and a linear monodromy map
\lambda:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
. The monodromy must be an isomorphism, and thus
\lambda\in\operatorname{GL}(1,\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{C}^{*}
, and also satisfy the additional constraint imposed by the cone singularity. By generically perturbing this singularity, it is readily seen that the condition is that the monodromy
\lambda
does not have
1
has an eigenvalue, which in this case reduces to
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
. This is precisely the flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(G)
.
\Box

Remark 6.1

(
[
Nad17b
,
TZ18
]
)
.

This particular wavefront allows for a direct Legendrian analysis of the Landau-Ginzburg model
(\mathbb{C}^{3},z_{1}z_{2}z_{3})
, as follows. The regular fiber
F\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{3}
of the superpotential is isomorphic to
F\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}
, and its Lagrangian skeleton is thus an exact 2-torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}\subseteq F
, i.e. the vanishing cycle for the (non-isolated) singularity
W
. Its Legendrian lift

\Lambda:=\{(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\in\mathbb{C}^{3}:|z_{1}|=|z_{2}|=|z_{3}|=1/3,\arg(z_{1})+\arg(z_{2})+\arg(z_{3})=0\}\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}}),

has vanishing (singular) thimble the conic Lagrangian

L=\{(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\in\mathbb{C}^{3}:W(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{+},|z_{1}|=|z_{2}|=|z_{3}|\}.

By performing a real blow-up at the origin, we introduce a real 2-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
at the origin and a projection map
\pi:\Lambda\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{2}
from our Legendrian 2-torus onto this exceptional 2-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. In coordinates, the map
\pi(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=(\Re(z_{1}),\Re(z_{2}),\Re(z_{3}))
is just given by taking the real parts of the complex coordinates and realizes the Legendrian surface
\Lambda\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
as the Legendrian weave
\iota(\Lambda(G))
associated to the four-vertex 2-graph
G\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{2}
, given by the 1-skeleton of the tetrahedron. Thus, the mirror of the Landau-Ginzburg model
(\mathbb{C}^{3},z_{1}z_{2}z_{3})
is the Legendrian 2-torus in
(J^{1}\mathbb{S}^{2},\xi_{\text{st}})
which satellites to the Clifford 2-torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{c}\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
. This leads to the description of the A-model Landau-Ginzburg model
(\mathbb{C}^{3},z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})
, given by the category
\mu\mbox{Sh}_{L}(\mathbb{C}^{3})
of wrapped sheaves, as the bounded dg-category of finitely-generated torsion complexes on the flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{c})\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
.
\Box

6.1.2.
The Double of the 4-Triangle

Let us consider the 4-graph
G(t_{4})
associated to a 4-triangle
t_{4}
, as depicted in Figure
80
(left), and described in Section
3
. Let
G=G(t_{4})\cup_{\partial}G(t_{4})\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{2}
be the 4-graph obtained by gluing two copies of this 4-graph along their boundaries, i.e.
G
is the 4-graph associated to the 4-triangulation of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
with two underlying
t_{1}
-triangles. The 4-graph
G
is depicted in Figure
80
(right), where the circle at the boundary is identified to a unique point, which is a hexagonal vertex.

Figure 80.
The local 4-graph
G(t_{4})
associated to a 4-triangle
t_{4}
(left). The global 4-graph
G(\tau_{4})
given by the 4-triangulation
\tau_{4}
of the 2-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
with two triangles.

For the computation of the flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(G)
, we employ our geometric techniques in Section
4
. Theorem
4.10
allows us to remove the initial three (blue)
\tau_{1}
-bigons, by considering a direct sum with three copies of the standard Legendrian 2-torus
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{st}
, see Section
4
. By applying Move I in Theorem
4.2
three times, we obtain the 3-graph in Figure
81
(left). Further removing three of the bigons, we reach the 3-graph
G_{0}
in Figure
81
(right). The framed flag moduli space
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(G_{0})
for the 3-graph
G_{0}
is given by the choice of two flags
\mathscr{F}_{1}=(p_{1},l_{1},\pi_{1}),\mathscr{F}_{2}=(p_{2},l_{2},\pi_{2})\in\operatorname{GL}_{4}/B
in projective 3-space, and a choice of three points
p_{3},p_{4},p_{5}\in\mathbb{P}_{k}^{3}
such that

-

(l_{1},\pi_{1})
and
(l_{2},\pi_{2})
are completely transverse, i.e.
l_{1}\not\in\pi_{2}
and
l_{2}\not\in\pi_{1}
, and
p_{1}\neq p_{2}
,

-

p_{3}\in l_{1}
,
p_{3}\neq p_{1}
,

-

p_{4}\in l_{2}
,
p_{3}\neq p_{2}
,

-

p_{5}\in\pi_{1}\cap\pi_{2}
,
p_{3}\neq p_{1}
.

In particular,
\mathcal{M}(G_{0})\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(G_{0})/\operatorname{PGL}_{4}
, and the flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(G_{0})
is described by the data above. By Theorem
4.10
, and the fact that each bigon contributes to
k^{*}
once the Legendrian weave is connected, we deduce that our original flag moduli space must be isomorphic to
\mathcal{M}(G)\cong\mathcal{M}(G_{0})\times(k^{*})^{4}
.

Figure 81.
The 4-graph
G(\tau_{4})
in Figure
80
after three index 1 anti-surgeries - accounted by the connected sums with
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{st}
- and simplified with Move I (right). The 4-graph obtained by three additional index anti-surgeries (right).

This simplification, from the original
4
-graph
G
to
G_{0}
, allows for a direct description above of the flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(G)
, from which further information can be readily extracted. For instance, the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-rational count for
\mathcal{M}(G)(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is immediately:

|\mathcal{M}(G)(\mathbb{F}_{q})|=\frac{(q-1)^{5}}{(q^{4}-1)(q^{4}-q)(q^{4}-q^{2})(q^{4}-q^{3})}\cdot q^{3}\cdot\frac{(q^{4}-1)(q^{3}-1)(q^{2}-1)}{(q-1)^{3}}(q+1)q,

as
|\mbox{PGL}(4,\mathbb{F}_{q})|=(q^{4}-1)(q^{4}-q)(q^{4}-q^{2})(q^{4}-q^{3})(q-1)^{-1}
, the rightmost multiplicative factor is the count for the two flags
\mathscr{F}_{1},\mathscr{F}_{2}
, and the
q^{3}
factors stands for the final choice of
(p_{3},p_{4},p_{5})
.
\Box

We conclude this initial gallery of computations with the following:

Example 6.2

(Concentric Circles)
.

Let
\tau=(\tau_{i_{1}},\tau_{i_{2}},\ldots,\tau_{i_{n}})
be an ordered collection of
n
simple transpositions
\tau_{i_{j}}\in S_{N-1}
,
1\leq j\leq n
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Consider the
N
-graph
G(\tau)\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{2}
described by
n
concentric circles
C_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{2}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, with center on the North Pole, and strictly increasing radius. This
N
-graph is depicted in Figure
82
(left).

Figure 82.
The
N
-graph
G(\tau)
associated to the sequence of transpositions
\tau=(\tau_{i_{1}},\tau_{i_{2}},\ldots,\tau_{i_{n}})
(left). The 4-graph
G(\tau)
associated to
\tau=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{3},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{1})
(right).

The Legendrian weave
\Lambda(G(\tau))\subseteq(J^{1}\mathbb{S}^{2},\xi_{\text{st}})
is a radial version of the
N
-stranded positive braid closure of
\beta=\sigma_{i_{1}}\sigma_{i_{2}}\cdot\ldots\cdot\sigma_{i_{n}}
. Smoothly, it is a link of
N
two-spheres
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. The moduli space of rank-one sheaves in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
supported along the positive braid
\beta
is the open Bott-Samelson variety
O(\beta)

[
STZ17
,
Tri19
,
CG20
]
.
By Section
5.2
, since
C=\mathbb{S}^{2}
is simply connected, there is no further monodromy information and
\mathcal{M}(G(\tau))=O(\beta)
.
In particular, the links with different
n
have a different number of points over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and cannot be Legendrian iosotopic.
We note further that
[
STZ17
, Theorem 6.34]
relates this number to the HOMFLY-PT polynomial of the (topological) knot in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
defined by the braid
\beta.

\Box

6.2.
Symmetry groups for Legendrian weaves

Let
\mathbb{G}
be an arbitrary finite group and
\Lambda\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
a Legendrian surface, with underlying smooth surface
S(\Lambda)
. Let
\mathcal{L}(\Lambda)
be the space of embedded Legendrian surfaces in
(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
Legendrian isotopic to the Legendrian surface
\Lambda
, with base point
\Lambda
. In addition, let
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda)
be the monoid of 3-dimensional exact Lagrangian concordances in the symplectization
(\mathbb{S}^{5}\times\mathbb{R}(t),e^{t}\lambda_{\text{st}})
, up to Hamiltonian isotopy, based on the Legendrian surface
\Lambda\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
. Let
\varphi_{t}:S(\Lambda)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
be a
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-family of Legendrian embeddings,
t\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Then the graph map

\mbox{gr}:\pi_{1}(\mathcal{L}(\Lambda))\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}(\Lambda),\quad[\varphi_{t}]\longmapsto(\varphi_{t}(S(\Lambda)),t),

allows us to relate loops of Legendrian surfaces with Lagrangian concordances.

These spaces
\mathcal{L}(\Lambda),\mathbb{L}(\Lambda)
are challenging to study. Already in the 1-dimensional case of Legendrian links
\Lambda\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{3},\xi_{\text{st}})
, it was only established recently that there exist Legendrian links such that the fundamental groups
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{L}(\Lambda))
can admit (infinite order) non-Abelian subgroups
[
CG20
, Corollary 1.6]
, and
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda)
actually contains elements of infinite order
[
CG20
, Corollary 1.7]
. To our knowledge, the only previous result about the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{L}(\Lambda))
or the monoid
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda)
for
\Lambda\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
a Legendrian surface was proven in
[
SS16
]
, where Legendrian surfaces
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}_{n}}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, were built such that
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{L}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}_{n}}))
admits the finite cyclic group
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
as a subgroup. Legendrian weaves and their flag moduli space are well-suited to address these questions. We present the following result for Legendrian surfaces in
(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
:

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be an arbitrary finite group. Then there exists a Legendrian surface
\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
such that

(i)

\mathbb{G}
is a subquotient of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{L}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}}))
,

(ii)

\mathbb{G}
is a subquotient of the 3-dimensional Lagrangian concordance monoid
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda_{\mathbb{G}})
.

In fact, the latter is the image of the former via the graph map
\mbox{gr}:\pi_{1}(\mathcal{L}(\Lambda))\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}(\Lambda)
.

Proof.

The argument is structured in two parts. First, we describe a construction of a 2-graph
G^{\prime}
given a triangulation of a surface. Second, we use this construction to prove the statement. The second part has itself two steps: in the first step, the statement is proven only for those finite groups
\mathbb{G}
which are Hurwitz groups
\mathbb{H}
.
20
20

20

A Hurwitz group
\mathbb{H}
is any finite group which can be generated by an element
x
of order 2 and an element
y
of order 3 whose product
xy
has order 7. Equivalently, a Hurwitz group is any finite nontrivial quotient of the
(2,3,7)
-triangle group
T(2,3,7):=\langle x,y\|x^{2}=y^{3}=(xy)^{7}=1\rangle
.
In the second step, the case of Hurwitz groups is used to conclude the statement for an arbitrary finite group.

First, we begin by describing a construction of
2
-graphs. Let
(C,T)
be a closed smooth surface,
T
a triangulation with
e(T)
edges, and
G(T)
the trivalent 2-graph dual to the triangulation
T
. Consider the 2-graph
G^{\prime}
obtained by adding a bigon at each edge of
G(T)
, using Move 4 in Figure
4
. By Theorem
4.10
, specifically Remark
4.11
.
(ii)
, the Legendrian
\iota(\Lambda(G^{\prime}))
is obtained by performing a connected sum of
\iota(\Lambda(G(T)))
with
e(T)
copies of the standard Legendrian torus
\mathbb{T}_{\text{st}}^{2}\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
. Then
[
DR11
, Proposition 4.6]
, or Theorem
5.12
, implies that the complex flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime})
is isomorphic to the product
\mathcal{M}(G(T))\times(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{e(T)}
, and thus
H^{*}(\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime}),\mathbb{Q})\cong H^{*}(\mathcal{M}(G(T),\mathbb{Q})\otimes H^{*}((\mathbb{C}^{*})^{e(T)},\mathbb{Q})
by the Künneth formula.

Second, we will now prove the statement in the case that
\mathbb{G}
is assumed to be an arbitrary but fixed Hurwitz group
\mathbb{H}
. By virtue of Hurwitz’ theorem
[
Hur92
,
LT99
]
, there exists a compact Riemann surface
C=C(\mathbb{H})
whose automorphism group is (isomorphic to)
\mathbb{H}
; this surface
C
is called a Hurwitz surface in the literature. The topological surface underlying the Riemann surface
C
admits a triangulation
T(\mathbb{H})
with symmetry group
\mathbb{H}
. In particular, the dual graph
G=G(T(\mathbb{H}))
also has symmetry group
\mathbb{H}
. Let us now consider the 2-graph
G^{\prime}
, associated to
G
as in the paragraph above, where the edge bigons are added such that
\mathbb{H}
is still a subgroup of the symmetry group of
G^{\prime}
. Note that, by construction,
\mathbb{H}
acts faithfully on the set of edges of the triangulation
T
, and thus
\mathbb{H}
also acts faithfully on the
1\otimes H^{*}((\mathbb{C}^{*})^{e(T)},\mathbb{Q})\subseteq H^{*}(\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime}),\mathbb{Q})
piece of the cohomology of the flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime})
.

Now, the generators
x,y
of the triangle group
T(2,3,7)
are geometrically given by rotations of the Poincaré hyperbolic disk, namely
x
is a rotation of angle
\pi
about the vertices of the
(2,3,7)
-Schwarz triangle and
y
corresponds to a rotation of angle
2\pi/3
. Since a rotation
\rho
is smoothly isotopic to the identity, as a diffeomorphism, there exists a contact isotopy
\phi_{t}(\rho)
,
t\in[0,1]
, of
(J^{1}C,\xi_{\text{st}})
such that
\phi_{0}(\rho)=id
and
\phi_{1}(\rho)
set-wise fixes the weave front associated to
G^{\prime}
, and thus the Legendrian surface
\Lambda(G^{\prime})
associated to it. This contact isotopy
\phi_{t}(\rho)
defines an element of
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{L}(\Lambda(G^{\prime})))
, and its graph an element of
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda(G^{\prime}))
. The flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime})
is a Legendrian isotopy invariant of the Legendrian surface
\Lambda(G^{\prime})\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{5},\xi_{\text{st}})
, and this contact isotopy induces an automorphism of
\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime})
. In particular, there are Legendrian isotopies
\phi_{t}(x)
and
\phi_{t}(y)
associated to the generators
x,y
of any Hurwitz group
\mathbb{H}
,
x
rotating
\pi
and
y
rotating
2\pi/3
. Thus the subgroup
\langle\phi_{t}(x),\phi_{t}(y)\rangle\subseteq\pi_{1}(\mathcal{L}(\Lambda(G^{\prime})))
acts by automorphisms in
\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime})
. Since
\mathbb{H}
acts faithfully in the cohomology
H^{*}(\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime}))
, as pointed out above,
\mathbb{H}
is a subquotient of
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{L}(\Lambda(G^{\prime})))
, namely, it is a quotient of the subgroup
\langle\phi_{t}(x),\phi_{t}(y)\rangle
. The argument for
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda(G^{\prime}))
is identical, and this concludes the required statement for Hurwitz groups
\mathbb{H}
.

Finally, to conclude the general statement, let
\mathbb{G}
be an arbitrary finite group and assume the result holds for Hurwitz groups, which is proven above. Then
\mathbb{G}
is a subgroup of the alternating group
A_{n}
for large enough
n\in\mathbb{N}
. By
[
Con84
, Section 3]
, see also
[
LT99
]
,
A_{n}
is a Hurwitz group
\mathbb{G}(C)
for
n\geq 168
, and thus
\mathbb{G}
injects into such a Hurwitz group
\mathbb{G}(C)
.
21
21

21

Note that
A_{n}
, for
n\leq 168
, is a subgroup of
A_{m}
, for a greater
m\geq n
, and thus all cases
A_{n}
are covered.
The argument above thus implies that
\mathbb{G}
is a subquotient for
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{L}(\Lambda(G^{\prime})))
and
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda(G^{\prime}))
. Hence, the choice of weave
\Lambda(G^{\prime})
completes the proof of Theorem
6.3
.
∎

We do not know whether or not a result analogous to Theorem
6.3
holds for 1-dimensional Legendrian knots
\Lambda\subseteq(\mathbb{S}^{3},\xi_{\text{st}})
. That could be a good question in low-dimensional contact topology. Any answer – positive or negative – would be of interest.

There is a complement to Theorem
6.3
for certain groups
\mathbb{G}
of infinite order, including non-Abelian groups such as
\mbox{PSL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
, by using results of the first author. Indeed, the Legendrian weave associated to the 4-graph
G(\tau)
with the eighteen concentric circles

\tau=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

represents a 3-component Legendrian link
\Lambda(G(\tau))
of
2
-spheres. The geometric
\mbox{Br}_{3}
-braid action constructed in
[
CG20
]
, modulo its center
Z(\mbox{Br}_{3})
, acts faithfully on the flag moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\Lambda(G(\tau)))
. This flag moduli space is described in Example
6.2
. Then
[
CG20
, Theorem 1.1]
shows that the modular group
\mbox{PSL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
acts faithfully on the cluster charts for the space obtained by forgetting the monodromies in the Grothendieck resolution
\mathcal{M}(\Lambda(G(\tau)))
. Hence,
\mbox{PSL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
is a subquotient of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{L}(\Lambda(G(\tau))))
and
\mathcal{L}(\Lambda(G(\tau)))
for these Legendrian weaves
\Lambda(G(\tau))
.

6.3.
Flag Moduli and Bipartite Graphs

In Section
3
, we introduced the construction of a 3-graph
G\subseteq C
associated to an embedded eponymous bipartite graph
G
. This subsection explains how to compute flag moduli spaces for such 3-graphs.

We will employ a useful notation, local to this subsection. If
a,b\in V^{3}
are distinct vectors in a 3-dimensional vector space
V
, we denote by
ab
the unique 2-plane spanned by
a,b
. Similarly, given two 2-planes
A,B,\subseteq V^{3}
, the intersection
A\cap B
will be denoted by
AB
.

At a hexagonal vertex, traveling between opposite faces requires crossing three edges of alternating colors, and thus opposite
faces are assigned completely transverse flags
\mathcal{A}=(a,A)=aA
and
\mathcal{B}=(b,B)=bB
. Note that a single such pair
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
determines the remaining four regions, by Lemma
5.6
: if crossing
red, blue, red
from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
, the flags in succession are
(a,A),(AB,A),(AB,B),(b,B)
. If crossing
blue, red, blue,
the flags are
(a,A),(a,ab),(b,ab),(b,B)
. This is depicted as follows:

(a,A)
(b,B)
(a,ab)
(AB,B)
(AB,A)
(b,ab)

\bullet
\bullet
a
b
AB
ab
A
B

Now consider an edge of the bicubic graph
G
. In the associated 3-graph, this edge generates two hexagonal vertices which are connected by two adjacent edges of different colors. This local configuration is said to be a hexagonal edge. Let us denote the two flags on opposite regions along the axis connecting the hexagonal vertices by
\mathcal{A}=aA
and
\mathcal{C}=cC
. Let
\mathcal{B}
be the flag in the interior region of the hexagonal edge, transverse to both
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{C}
. There are two further conditions on the flag
\mathcal{B}
:

\quad AB\subset C,\qquad c\subset ab.

The Weyl group
W(A_{2})\cong S_{3}
is the symmetric group on three elements, and thus there are six possible relative positions for the two flags
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{GL}_{3}/B
. Here we consider the case of a finite field
k=\mathbb{F}_{q}
. In a hexagonal edge, the relative position of the two outer flags
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}
is restricted:

Lemma 6.4
.

The two outer flags
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}
in a hexagonal edge must coincide or be completely transverse. In addition, with
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}
fixed, number the of choices of flag
\mathcal{B}
in the interior of the hexagonal edge is
q^{3}
, in the case
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{C}
, and
q-1
, in the case
\mathcal{A}\neq\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

Let us analyze their possible relative positions, labeled according to the elements
W(A_{2})=\{0,1,2,12,21,121\}
:

-

Type 0:
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{A}.

Then the conditions are automatic, and
\mathcal{B}
is
simply transverse to
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{C}.

There are
q^{3}
such choices.

-

Type 1:
c=a,C\neq A.
The second condition is then automatic, but
C\supset a=c
and
C\supset AB
means
C=A.
This is a contradiction.

-

Type 2:
c\neq a,C=A.
The first condition is then automatic, but
c\subset C=A
and
c\subset ab
means
c=a
.
This is a contradiction.

-

Type 12:
a\neq c,C\neq A
but
a\subset C
. Then
a\subset C
and
AB\subset C
means
C=A

This is a contradiction.

-

Type 21:
a\neq c,C\neq A
but
c\subset A.
Then
c\subset A
and
c\subset ab
means
c=a.

This is a contradiction.

-

Type 121: In this case, the flag
\mathcal{B}
is determined by
either
equivalent choice: a line
b
in
ac
not equal to
a
or
c
(then
B
is the plane
bAC
) or a plane
B
containing
AC

not equal to
A
or
C
(then
b
is
acB
).
The number of such choices is
q-1
.

Therefore, this flag
\mathcal{B}
has either
q^{3}
or
q-1
internal degrees of freedom, respectively, after fixing the outer flags
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{C}
to be
either equal or completely transverse. The other configurations have no solutions.
∎

We now apply Lemma
6.4
and the discussion above to prove Theorem
1.5
in the introduction.

6.4.
Non-isotopic Links of Legendrian Spheres

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and consider the bipartite Ladder Graph
L_{n}\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{2}
depicted in Figure
83
(bottom). The number
n\in\mathbb{N}
denotes half the number of square faces, and the right and left sides of the bipartite graph are identified in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. In particular,
\mathbb{S}^{2}\setminus L_{n}
has
2n+2
connected components,
2n
squares and two 2-disks, at the north and south poles of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. We consider its associated 3-graph
\mathcal{L}_{n}\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{2}
, as described in Section
3
, which is shown in Figure
83
(bottom). The Legendrian weave
\Lambda(\mathcal{L}_{n})\subseteq(J^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{2}),\xi_{\text{st}})
consists of a 3-component link of Legendrian 2-spheres, independent of
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Figure 83.
The bipartite Ladder Graph
L_{n}
, where the right and left sides are identified after
n
rungs (bottom). The 3-graph
\mathcal{L}_{n}
associated to
L_{n}
(top).

Note that the Legendrian link
\Lambda(\mathcal{L}_{n})\subseteq(J^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{2}),\xi_{\text{st}})
is smoothly isotopic to the surface unlink, as the codimension of this smooth embedding is three. We now show that the Legendrian isotopy type of the Legendrian link
\Lambda(\mathcal{L}_{n})\subseteq(J^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{2}),\xi_{\text{st}})
is different for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. This will be achieved by counting the number of points of their flag moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{L}_{n})
over a finite field. The precise statement reads:

Theorem 6.5

(Theorem
1.5
)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{n}\subseteq\mathbb{S}^{2}
be the
(2n)
-runged ladder graph and
\mathbb{F}_{q}
a finite field,
q
a prime power. Then the flag moduli space
